People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, it’s a long story, really. I came in because, well, my wife told me I should, but I also just need someone to understand what’s been happening. You see, I go down to the park most afternoons to play dominoes with the fellas—have been for years, ever since I stopped working. It’s a good group, mostly, but lately, I’ve been noticing things. The men at the table, they don't say things outright, but they drop these little hints, these messages that are meant just for me. It’s in the way they place a tile, or a certain phrase they use about the weather, but I know it’s a warning. And it’s not just them, either. Sometimes I’ll be sitting in my armchair at home, listening to the radio—I like the old-time music stations, you know—and the announcer will say something between songs. To anyone else, it sounds like he’s just talking about the next track or a commercial, but I can hear the hidden meaning. He’s telling me to be careful, to watch my back. My wife, she just waves her hand and says, "Oh, it’s just talk, you’re reading too much into it," but she doesn't hear it the way I do. She doesn't understand that they are trying to warn me. I know they are. I just... I need to figure out what it is I'm supposed to be so careful about, because it's starting to weigh on my mind all day long.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Memory? Well, now, I wouldn't say that at all. My wife, she might fuss and complain that I forget to put my coffee cup in the sink or that I left the newspaper on the porch, but that is just because my mind is occupied with more important matters, like those warnings I was telling you about. My actual memory is sharp as a tack, doctor. 

Why, just yesterday down at the park, I could tell you exactly which tiles old George had played three hands ago, and I remembered that Henry owed me fifty cents from last Thursday. I can remember things from way back, too, clear as day. I remember when I was just a boy, long before I had to leave school to help out with the work, I can still picture the exact layout of the classroom and the color of the teacher's dress on my last day. I remember all the fellas I worked with over the years, their names, their faces, the stories they told. 

So no, my mind is holding onto things just fine. Honestly, sometimes I think my memory is almost too good, because I hold onto all these little details that other folks just breeze right past. If my memory was going bad, I wouldn't catch those hidden messages on the radio, would I? I wouldn't remember that the announcer used that exact same strange tone of voice last Tuesday right before the three o'clock news, or that he played the same sequence of songs the day before that. I keep track of all of it in my head. So the memory is working perfectly, it is just... well, it is just that I have a whole lot on my mind to remember these days, what with having to stay so alert all the time and piece together what they want from me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A head injury? Oh, goodness no, doctor. No, no, my head is perfectly fine. I haven't taken a fall or hit my noggin since... well, I suppose since I was a young man working on the loading docks back in, oh, must have been '58 or '59. A crate swung loose and clipped me, but that was just a bump, nothing serious at all. I just dusted myself off and went right back to work. 

And as for illnesses, well, I'm eighty-four years old, so sure, my knees get a bit stiff when the weather turns cold, and I take a little pill for my blood pressure that the clinic gave me a few years back, but I haven't had any strokes or brain troubles or anything major like that. My body is holding up just fine for a man my age. I eat a good breakfast every morning—my wife makes sure of that, always fixes me oatmeal with just a pinch of brown sugar, keeps the engine running, so to speak. 

So no, it's not a medical thing. That's what my wife tries to say sometimes, she says, "Oh, you're just getting old, your body is wearing down and your mind is playing tricks," but I tell her, my brain is as healthy as a horse. These things I'm hearing, the messages on the radio and the way the fellas look at me down at the park, they didn't start because I got sick. They didn't start because of a head injury. It started because... well, because something is actually going on out there. It's real, you see? It's not some sickness in my head, it's just that I'm finally paying attention to the signs they're giving me, and I just wish folks would realize I'm seeing things clearly instead of asking if I bumped my head.

It’s in the way they place a tile, or a certain phrase they use about the weather, but I know it’s a warning. And it’s not just them, either. It's not some sickness in my head, it's just that I'm finally paying attention to the signs they're giving me, and I just wish folks would realize I'm seeing things clearly instead of asking if I bumped my head.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you are not listening to the radio, do you ever hear a voice speaking to you that other people cannot hear?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 84 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Hallucinations\nmanifestation: \"Some afternoons, when the house is quiet, I hear my old friend call my name from the porch or hum a choir song, clear like he is standing right there.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Voices? Well, now, that is a funny thing to ask, doctor, because I was just thinking about this very thing the other day. I tried telling my wife about it, but she just brushed it off, the way she does when she's busy with her shows. But you see, it happens mostly in the afternoons. Some afternoons, when the house gets real quiet—maybe the radio is switched off for once, and my wife has gone down to the market to get a few things for supper, or maybe she's just in the back room taking a nap—I'll be sitting there in my chair, just resting my eyes and enjoying the peace and quiet. 

And then I'll hear it. Clear as a bell, doctor. It is my old friend. I'll hear him call my name right from the front porch, just as plain as day, like he's coming up the wooden steps to sit a spell and chew the fat. Sometimes he doesn't call my name, though. Sometimes he is just humming. He used to sing in the church choir, you know, had this deep, rich voice that could really carry, so I'll hear him humming one of those old choir songs. It's just a low, steady tune, but it is so clear, clear like he is standing right there next to me in the living room. 

I always get up, you know, I push myself up out of the chair—which takes a minute these days—and I go look out the front window, expecting to see him standing out there on the porch. But there is never anybody there. Just the empty porch and the street, maybe a neighbor's dog walking by, but no sign of him. It's the strangest thing, doctor, but it is so real. I know what I hear. My ears are as good as my memory, and I would know that voice anywhere. I always sit back down and I think about it for a good long while, trying to figure out if it's another kind of message, like the ones the fellas or the radio announcer are sending me, or if maybe sound just travels funny on a quiet afternoon. But I definitely hear him, just as clear as I'm hearing you right now.

The men at the table, they don't say things outright, but they drop these little hints, these messages that are meant just for me. It’s in the way they place a tile, or a certain phrase they use about the weather, but I know it’s a warning. And it’s not just them, either. Sometimes I’ll be sitting in my armchair at home, listening to the radio—I like the old-time music stations, you know—and the announcer will say something between songs. To anyone else, it sounds like he’s just talking about the next track or a commercial, but I can hear the hidden meaning. He’s telling me to be careful, to watch my back. My wife, she just waves her hand and says, \"Oh, it’s just talk, you’re reading too much into it,\" but she doesn't hear it the way I do. She doesn't understand that they are trying to warn me. I know they are. I just... I need to figure out what it is I'm supposed to be so careful about, because it's starting to weigh on my mind all day long.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any worsening problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? Well, now, I wouldn't say that at all. My wife, she might fuss and complain that I forget to put my coffee cup in the sink or that I left the newspaper on the porch, but that is just because my mind is occupied with more important matters, like those warnings I was telling you about. My actual memory is sharp as a tack, doctor. \n\nWhy, just yesterday down at the park, I could tell you exactly which tiles old George had played three hands ago, and I remembered that Henry owed me fifty cents from last Thursday. I can remember things from way back, too, clear as day. I remember when I was just a boy, long before I had to leave school to help out with the work, I can still picture the exact layout of the classroom and the color of the teacher's dress on my last day. I remember all the fellas I worked with over the years, their names, their faces, the stories they told. \n\nSo no, my mind is holding onto things just fine. Honestly, sometimes I think my memory is almost too good, because I hold onto all these little details that other folks just breeze right past. If my memory was going bad, I wouldn't catch those hidden messages on the radio, would I? I wouldn't remember that the announcer used that exact same strange tone of voice last Tuesday right before the three o'clock news, or that he played the same sequence of songs the day before that. I keep track of all of it in my head. A crate swung loose and clipped me, but that was just a bump, nothing serious at all. I just dusted myself off and went right back to work. \n\nAnd as for illnesses, well, I'm eighty-four years old, so sure, my knees get a bit stiff when the weather turns cold, and I take a little pill for my blood pressure that the clinic gave me a few years back, but I haven't had any strokes or brain troubles or anything major like that. My body is holding up just fine for a man my age. I eat a good breakfast every morning—my wife makes sure of that, always fixes me oatmeal with just a pinch of brown sugar, keeps the engine running, so to speak. \n\nSo no, it's not a medical thing. That's what my wife tries to say sometimes, she says, \"Oh, you're just getting old, your body is wearing down and your mind is playing tricks,\" but I tell her, my brain is as healthy as a horse. These things I'm hearing, the messages on the radio and the way the fellas look at me down at the park, they didn't start because I got sick. They didn't start because of a head injury. Some afternoons, when the house gets real quiet—maybe the radio is switched off for once, and my wife has gone down to the market to get a few things for supper, or maybe she's just in the back room taking a nap—I'll be sitting there in my chair, just resting my eyes and enjoying the peace and quiet. \n\nAnd then I'll hear it. Clear as a bell, doctor. It is my old friend. I'll hear him call my name right from the front porch, just as plain as day, like he's coming up the wooden steps to sit a spell and chew the fat. Sometimes he doesn't call my name, though. Sometimes he is just humming. He used to sing in the church choir, you know, had this deep, rich voice that could really carry, so I'll hear him humming one of those old choir songs. It's just a low, steady tune, but it is so clear, clear like he is standing right there next to me in the living room. \n\nI always get up, you know, I push myself up out of the chair—which takes a minute these days—and I go look out the front window, expecting to see him standing out there on the porch. But there is never anybody there. Just the empty porch and the street, maybe a neighbor's dog walking by, but no sign of him. It's the strangest thing, doctor, but it is so real. I know what I hear. My ears are as good as my memory, and I would know that voice anywhere. I always sit back down and I think about it for a good long while, trying to figure out if it's another kind of message, like the ones the fellas or the radio announcer are sending me, or if maybe sound just travels funny on a quiet afternoon. But I definitely hear him, just as clear as I'm hearing you right now.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been experiencing these messages and hearing your friend’s voice?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

If my memory was going bad, I wouldn't catch those hidden messages on the radio, would I? I wouldn't remember that the announcer used that exact same strange tone of voice last Tuesday right before the three o'clock news, or that he played the same sequence of songs the day before that. I keep track of all of it in my head. So the memory is working perfectly, it is just... well, it is just that I have a whole lot on my mind to remember these days, what with having to stay so alert all the time and piece together what they want from me.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences begin after you developed a medical or neurological illness, or after a significant head injury?\n\nPatient: A head injury? Oh, goodness no, doctor. No, no, my head is perfectly fine. I just dusted myself off and went right back to work. \n\nAnd as for illnesses, well, I'm eighty-four years old, so sure, my knees get a bit stiff when the weather turns cold, and I take a little pill for my blood pressure that the clinic gave me a few years back, but I haven't had any strokes or brain troubles or anything major like that. My body is holding up just fine for a man my age. I eat a good breakfast every morning—my wife makes sure of that, always fixes me oatmeal with just a pinch of brown sugar, keeps the engine running, so to speak. \n\nSo no, it's not a medical thing. That's what my wife tries to say sometimes, she says, \"Oh, you're just getting old, your body is wearing down and your mind is playing tricks,\" but I tell her, my brain is as healthy as a horse. These things I'm hearing, the messages on the radio and the way the fellas look at me down at the park, they didn't start because I got sick. They didn't start because of a head injury. It started because... well, because something is actually going on out there. It's real, you see? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, now, let me think back on that, doctor. Time is a funny thing when you get to be my age, the days sort of blend together, you know? But I've been keeping track, I always keep track. It started back before the winter really set in. I remember because the leaves were just starting to turn that nice golden color on the big oak tree out front—the one my father planted when I was just a boy, if you can believe that. So that would make it... oh, about nine months now. 

Yes, nine months of it, off and on. It's not every single minute of the day, you understand. Sometimes a whole week will go by and the radio just plays regular music, and the fellas at the park are just playing dominos, and the house is perfectly quiet in the afternoons. But then, just when I think things have settled down, there it is again. The announcer drops his voice, or I hear my old friend humming that choir tune from the porch. It comes and goes, but it's been happening for a good nine months. I told my wife, I said, "Martha, it's been going on since before Thanksgiving," but she just told me to eat my dinner and stop talking nonsense. But it's not nonsense, doctor. Nine months is a long time to be getting these signs, a long time to be on alert. It makes a man tired, I'll tell you that much, always trying to put the pieces together. But I've been doing it for nine months, and I'll keep doing it as long as I have to.

It comes and goes, but it's been happening for a good nine months. I told my wife, I said, \"Martha, it's been going on since before Thanksgiving,\" but she just told me to eat my dinner and stop talking nonsense. But it's not nonsense, doctor. Nine months is a long time to be getting these signs, a long time to be on alert. It makes a man tired, I'll tell you that much, always trying to put the pieces together. But I've been doing it for nine months, and I'll keep doing it as long as I have to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, now, that’s... that’s a complicated question, doctor. You see, Martha, she’s always been the one to keep the house running like a top, so I don’t have much to do with the cooking or the cleaning, thank goodness for that. But as for my own things, the things I’ve always done to keep busy since I retired from the docks... well, things have changed a bit. I suppose you could say they’ve slowed down. 

Take my garden out back, for instance. I’ve always had the best tomatoes on the block, big red ones, and I used to spend hours out there pulling weeds and tying up the vines. But lately... well, with all the messages I’m getting, and trying to figure out what they mean, I just haven’t had the energy or the focus to get out there like I used to. Parts of it have gone completely to seed, just overgrown with crabgrass and dandelions. Martha fusses about it, but I tell her I have more important things on my mind right now. 

And then there’s the choir. I’ve sung bass in the First Methodist choir for forty years, rain or shine. But these last few months, I’ve missed quite a few rehearsals. Sometimes I’ll be getting my coat on to go, and then I’ll hear the radio announcer drop his voice, or I’ll think about my old friend humming that tune on the porch, and I just... I just have to sit back down and think on it. I can’t be standing up there in the choir loft singing hymns when I’m trying to decipher what’s really going on, you know? 

And the fellas at the park... well, I used to go down there every Tuesday and Thursday afternoon for dominos. We had a regular foursome. But ever since they started looking at me funny, sending me those signs with their eyes and the way they tap the tiles... well, I called them out on it a few times. I said, "I know what you’re doing, I know what you’re trying to tell me." And they just looked at me like I was speaking Greek. It got... uncomfortable, I suppose is the word. Very uncomfortable. So I just don’t join in like I used to. I’ll walk down to the park, maybe sit on a bench a little ways off and watch them, but I don’t sit at the table anymore. It’s just too much to keep track of, doctor, trying to play a game and read all those hidden messages at the same time. So yes, I suppose my routine has shifted quite a bit since all this started.

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it’s a long story, really. I came in because, well, my wife told me I should, but I also just need someone to understand what’s been happening. You see, I go down to the park most afternoons to play dominoes with the fellas—have been for years, ever since I stopped working. It’s a good group, mostly, but lately, I’ve been noticing things. The men at the table, they don't say things outright, but they drop these little hints, these messages that are meant just for me. It’s in the way they place a tile, or a certain phrase they use about the weather, but I know it’s a warning. And it’s not just them, either. Sometimes I’ll be sitting in my armchair at home, listening to the radio—I like the old-time music stations, you know—and the announcer will say something between songs. To anyone else, it sounds like he’s just talking about the next track or a commercial, but I can hear the hidden meaning. He’s telling me to be careful, to watch my back. My wife, she just waves her hand and says, \"Oh, it’s just talk, you’re reading too much into it,\" but she doesn't hear it the way I do. She doesn't understand that they are trying to warn me. I know they are. I just... I need to figure out what it is I'm supposed to be so careful about, because it's starting to weigh on my mind all day long.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any worsening problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? I can remember things from way back, too, clear as day. I remember when I was just a boy, long before I had to leave school to help out with the work, I can still picture the exact layout of the classroom and the color of the teacher's dress on my last day. I remember all the fellas I worked with over the years, their names, their faces, the stories they told. \n\nSo no, my mind is holding onto things just fine. I tried telling my wife about it, but she just brushed it off, the way she does when she's busy with her shows. But you see, it happens mostly in the afternoons. Some afternoons, when the house gets real quiet—maybe the radio is switched off for once, and my wife has gone down to the market to get a few things for supper, or maybe she's just in the back room taking a nap—I'll be sitting there in my chair, just resting my eyes and enjoying the peace and quiet. \n\nAnd then I'll hear it. Clear as a bell, doctor. It is my old friend. I'll hear him call my name right from the front porch, just as plain as day, like he's coming up the wooden steps to sit a spell and chew the fat. Sometimes he doesn't call my name, though. Sometimes he is just humming. He used to sing in the church choir, you know, had this deep, rich voice that could really carry, so I'll hear him humming one of those old choir songs. It's just a low, steady tune, but it is so clear, clear like he is standing right there next to me in the living room. \n\nI always get up, you know, I push myself up out of the chair—which takes a minute these days—and I go look out the front window, expecting to see him standing out there on the porch. But there is never anybody there. Just the empty porch and the street, maybe a neighbor's dog walking by, but no sign of him. It's the strangest thing, doctor, but it is so real. I know what I hear. My ears are as good as my memory, and I would know that voice anywhere. I always sit back down and I think about it for a good long while, trying to figure out if it's another kind of message, like the ones the fellas or the radio announcer are sending me, or if maybe sound just travels funny on a quiet afternoon. But I definitely hear him, just as clear as I'm hearing you right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these messages and hearing your friend’s voice?\n\nPatient: Well, now, let me think back on that, doctor. Time is a funny thing when you get to be my age, the days sort of blend together, you know? But I've been keeping track, I always keep track. It started back before the winter really set in. I remember because the leaves were just starting to turn that nice golden color on the big oak tree out front—the one my father planted when I was just a boy, if you can believe that. So that would make it... oh, about nine months now. \n\nYes, nine months of it, off and on. It's not every single minute of the day, you understand. Sometimes a whole week will go by and the radio just plays regular music, and the fellas at the park are just playing dominos, and the house is perfectly quiet in the afternoons. But then, just when I think things have settled down, there it is again. The announcer drops his voice, or I hear my old friend humming that choir tune from the porch. It comes and goes, but it's been happening for a good nine months. I told my wife, I said, \"Martha, it's been going on since before Thanksgiving,\" but she just told me to eat my dinner and stop talking nonsense. But it's not nonsense, doctor. Nine months is a long time to be getting these signs, a long time to be on alert. It makes a man tired, I'll tell you that much, always trying to put the pieces together. But I've been doing it for nine months, and I'll keep doing it as long as I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any decline in your ability to manage your usual daily activities since these experiences began?\n\nPatient: Well, now, that’s... that’s a complicated question, doctor. You see, Martha, she’s always been the one to keep the house running like a top, so I don’t have much to do with the cooking or the cleaning, thank goodness for that. But as for my own things, the things I’ve always done to keep busy since I retired from the docks... well, things have changed a bit. I suppose you could say they’ve slowed down. \n\nTake my garden out back, for instance. I’ve always had the best tomatoes on the block, big red ones, and I used to spend hours out there pulling weeds and tying up the vines. But lately... well, with all the messages I’m getting, and trying to figure out what they mean, I just haven’t had the energy or the focus to get out there like I used to. Parts of it have gone completely to seed, just overgrown with crabgrass and dandelions. Martha fusses about it, but I tell her I have more important things on my mind right now. \n\nAnd then there’s the choir. I’ve sung bass in the First Methodist choir for forty years, rain or shine. But these last few months, I’ve missed quite a few rehearsals. Sometimes I’ll be getting my coat on to go, and then I’ll hear the radio announcer drop his voice, or I’ll think about my old friend humming that tune on the porch, and I just... I just have to sit back down and think on it. I can’t be standing up there in the choir loft singing hymns when I’m trying to decipher what’s really going on, you know? \n\nAnd the fellas at the park... well, I used to go down there every Tuesday and Thursday afternoon for dominos. We had a regular foursome. But ever since they started looking at me funny, sending me those signs with their eyes and the way they tap the tiles... well, I called them out on it a few times. I said, \"I know what you’re doing, I know what you’re trying to tell me.\" And they just looked at me like I was speaking Greek. It got... uncomfortable, I suppose is the word. Very uncomfortable. So I just don’t join in like I used to. I’ll walk down to the park, maybe sit on a bench a little ways off and watch them, but I don’t sit at the table anymore. It’s just too much to keep track of, doctor, trying to play a game and read all those hidden messages at the same time. So yes, I suppose my routine has shifted quite a bit since all this started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Well, now, that is a funny thing to ask, doctor, because I was just thinking about this very thing the other day. I tried telling my wife about it, but she just brushed it off, the way she does when she's busy with her shows. But you see, it happens mostly in the afternoons. Some afternoons, when the house gets real quiet—maybe the radio is switched off for once, and my wife has gone down to the market to get a few things for supper, or maybe she's just in the back room taking a nap—I'll be sitting there in my chair, just resting my eyes and enjoying the peace and quiet. \n\nAnd then I'll hear it. Clear as a bell, doctor. It is my old friend. I'll hear him call my name right from the front porch, just as plain as day, like he's coming up the wooden steps to sit a spell and chew the fat. Sometimes he doesn't call my name, though. Sometimes he is just humming. He used to sing in the church choir, you know, had this deep, rich voice that could really carry, so I'll hear him humming one of those old choir songs. It's just a low, steady tune, but it is so clear, clear like he is standing right there next to me in the living room. \n\nI always get up, you know, I push myself up out of the chair—which takes a minute these days—and I go look out the front window, expecting to see him standing out there on the porch. But there is never anybody there. Just the empty porch and the street, maybe a neighbor's dog walking by, but no sign of him. It's the strangest thing, doctor, but it is so real. I know what I hear. My ears are as good as my memory, and I would know that voice anywhere. I always sit back down and I think about it for a good long while, trying to figure out if it's another kind of message, like the ones the fellas or the radio announcer are sending me, or if maybe sound just travels funny on a quiet afternoon. But I definitely hear him, just as clear as I'm hearing you right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these messages and hearing your friend’s voice?\n\nPatient: Well, now, let me think back on that, doctor. Time is a funny thing when you get to be my age, the days sort of blend together, you know? But I've been keeping track, I always keep track. It started back before the winter really set in. I remember because the leaves were just starting to turn that nice golden color on the big oak tree out front—the one my father planted when I was just a boy, if you can believe that. So that would make it... oh, about nine months now. \n\nYes, nine months of it, off and on. It's not every single minute of the day, you understand. Sometimes a whole week will go by and the radio just plays regular music, and the fellas at the park are just playing dominos, and the house is perfectly quiet in the afternoons. But then, just when I think things have settled down, there it is again. The announcer drops his voice, or I hear my old friend humming that choir tune from the porch. It comes and goes, but it's been happening for a good nine months. I told my wife, I said, \"Martha, it's been going on since before Thanksgiving,\" but she just told me to eat my dinner and stop talking nonsense. But it's not nonsense, doctor. Nine months is a long time to be getting these signs, a long time to be on alert. It makes a man tired, I'll tell you that much, always trying to put the pieces together. But I've been doing it for nine months, and I'll keep doing it as long as I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any decline in your ability to manage your usual daily activities since these experiences began?\n\nPatient: Well, now, that’s... that’s a complicated question, doctor. You see, Martha, she’s always been the one to keep the house running like a top, so I don’t have much to do with the cooking or the cleaning, thank goodness for that. But as for my own things, the things I’ve always done to keep busy since I retired from the docks... well, things have changed a bit. I suppose you could say they’ve slowed down. \n\nTake my garden out back, for instance. I’ve always had the best tomatoes on the block, big red ones, and I used to spend hours out there pulling weeds and tying up the vines. But lately... well, with all the messages I’m getting, and trying to figure out what they mean, I just haven’t had the energy or the focus to get out there like I used to. Parts of it have gone completely to seed, just overgrown with crabgrass and dandelions. Martha fusses about it, but I tell her I have more important things on my mind right now. \n\nAnd then there’s the choir. I’ve sung bass in the First Methodist choir for forty years, rain or shine. But these last few months, I’ve missed quite a few rehearsals. Sometimes I’ll be getting my coat on to go, and then I’ll hear the radio announcer drop his voice, or I’ll think about my old friend humming that tune on the porch, and I just... I just have to sit back down and think on it. I can’t be standing up there in the choir loft singing hymns when I’m trying to decipher what’s really going on, you know? \n\nAnd the fellas at the park... well, I used to go down there every Tuesday and Thursday afternoon for dominos. We had a regular foursome. But ever since they started looking at me funny, sending me those signs with their eyes and the way they tap the tiles... well, I called them out on it a few times. I said, \"I know what you’re doing, I know what you’re trying to tell me.\" And they just looked at me like I was speaking Greek. It got... uncomfortable, I suppose is the word. Very uncomfortable. So I just don’t join in like I used to. I’ll walk down to the park, maybe sit on a bench a little ways off and watch them, but I don’t sit at the table anymore. It’s just too much to keep track of, doctor, trying to play a game and read all those hidden messages at the same time. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, now, doctor, Martha tells me I’m getting awful hard to follow these days. Even when I’m just trying to talk about regular things, everyday things, you know? Not the messages or the radio or the fellas at the park, just normal, everyday talk. Like just the other morning, I sat down at the kitchen table with my coffee—black, just the way I've always taken it since my days working down on the docks—and I wanted to tell her about the back screen door needing a new spring, or maybe it was the gutters needing clearing out before the spring rains. Just a simple house repair, nothing complicated. 

But before I could get the words out the way I wanted, my mind just took off on me. It’s like a runaway train up there sometimes, just clicking and clacking down the tracks without stopping at any of the stations. My mind runs from the choir to baseball to repairs to my friend and back again so fast that I cannot hold one thought long enough to explain it right. I’ll be trying to tell Martha about that squeaky door, and suddenly I’m thinking about the hymns we sang at the First Methodist last Easter, and then my brain jumps right over to the old baseball games we used to listen to on the AM dial, you know, the real greats playing back in the fifties, and then it swings right back around to my old friend humming on the porch. 

It all just spins and spins together in this big rush. I try to catch just one of those thoughts, just grab onto one so I can speak it plain to my wife, but they move too quick. I end up starting a sentence about needing a screwdriver and finishing it with a story about a double play or a choir robe, and poor Martha just throws her hands up in the air, tells me I'm talking in circles, and goes back to washing the dishes. So, I suppose the honest answer is no, it doesn’t stay very clear. Even when I want to just have a regular chat, it’s just too crowded and moving too fast to keep it all straight and organized.

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it’s a long story, really. I came in because, well, my wife told me I should, but I also just need someone to understand what’s been happening. You see, I go down to the park most afternoons to play dominoes with the fellas—have been for years, ever since I stopped working. It’s a good group, mostly, but lately, I’ve been noticing things. The men at the table, they don't say things outright, but they drop these little hints, these messages that are meant just for me. It’s in the way they place a tile, or a certain phrase they use about the weather, but I know it’s a warning. And it’s not just them, either. Sometimes I’ll be sitting in my armchair at home, listening to the radio—I like the old-time music stations, you know—and the announcer will say something between songs. To anyone else, it sounds like he’s just talking about the next track or a commercial, but I can hear the hidden meaning. He’s telling me to be careful, to watch my back. My wife, she just waves her hand and says, \"Oh, it’s just talk, you’re reading too much into it,\" but she doesn't hear it the way I do. She doesn't understand that they are trying to warn me. I haven't taken a fall or hit my noggin since... well, I suppose since I was a young man working on the loading docks back in, oh, must have been '58 or '59. A crate swung loose and clipped me, but that was just a bump, nothing serious at all. I just dusted myself off and went right back to work. \n\nAnd as for illnesses, well, I'm eighty-four years old, so sure, my knees get a bit stiff when the weather turns cold, and I take a little pill for my blood pressure that the clinic gave me a few years back, but I haven't had any strokes or brain troubles or anything major like that. My body is holding up just fine for a man my age. I eat a good breakfast every morning—my wife makes sure of that, always fixes me oatmeal with just a pinch of brown sugar, keeps the engine running, so to speak. \n\nSo no, it's not a medical thing. That's what my wife tries to say sometimes, she says, \"Oh, you're just getting old, your body is wearing down and your mind is playing tricks,\" but I tell her, my brain is as healthy as a horse. These things I'm hearing, the messages on the radio and the way the fellas look at me down at the park, they didn't start because I got sick. They didn't start because of a head injury. It started because... well, because something is actually going on out there. It's real, you see? It's not some sickness in my head, it's just that I'm finally paying attention to the signs they're giving me, and I just wish folks would realize I'm seeing things clearly instead of asking if I bumped my head.\n\nDoctor: When you are not listening to the radio, do you ever hear a voice speaking to you that other people cannot hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? Well, now, that is a funny thing to ask, doctor, because I was just thinking about this very thing the other day. I tried telling my wife about it, but she just brushed it off, the way she does when she's busy with her shows. But you see, it happens mostly in the afternoons. Some afternoons, when the house gets real quiet—maybe the radio is switched off for once, and my wife has gone down to the market to get a few things for supper, or maybe she's just in the back room taking a nap—I'll be sitting there in my chair, just resting my eyes and enjoying the peace and quiet. \n\nAnd then I'll hear it. Clear as a bell, doctor. It is my old friend. I'll hear him call my name right from the front porch, just as plain as day, like he's coming up the wooden steps to sit a spell and chew the fat. Sometimes he doesn't call my name, though. Sometimes he is just humming. He used to sing in the church choir, you know, had this deep, rich voice that could really carry, so I'll hear him humming one of those old choir songs. It's just a low, steady tune, but it is so clear, clear like he is standing right there next to me in the living room. \n\nI always get up, you know, I push myself up out of the chair—which takes a minute these days—and I go look out the front window, expecting to see him standing out there on the porch. But there is never anybody there. Just the empty porch and the street, maybe a neighbor's dog walking by, but no sign of him. It's the strangest thing, doctor, but it is so real. I know what I hear. My ears are as good as my memory, and I would know that voice anywhere. I always sit back down and I think about it for a good long while, trying to figure out if it's another kind of message, like the ones the fellas or the radio announcer are sending me, or if maybe sound just travels funny on a quiet afternoon. But I definitely hear him, just as clear as I'm hearing you right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these messages and hearing your friend’s voice?\n\nPatient: Well, now, let me think back on that, doctor. Time is a funny thing when you get to be my age, the days sort of blend together, you know? But I've been keeping track, I always keep track. It started back before the winter really set in. I remember because the leaves were just starting to turn that nice golden color on the big oak tree out front—the one my father planted when I was just a boy, if you can believe that. So that would make it... oh, about nine months now. \n\nYes, nine months of it, off and on. It's not every single minute of the day, you understand. Sometimes a whole week will go by and the radio just plays regular music, and the fellas at the park are just playing dominos, and the house is perfectly quiet in the afternoons. But then, just when I think things have settled down, there it is again. The announcer drops his voice, or I hear my old friend humming that choir tune from the porch. It comes and goes, but it's been happening for a good nine months. I told my wife, I said, \"Martha, it's been going on since before Thanksgiving,\" but she just told me to eat my dinner and stop talking nonsense. But it's not nonsense, doctor. Nine months is a long time to be getting these signs, a long time to be on alert. It makes a man tired, I'll tell you that much, always trying to put the pieces together. But I've been doing it for nine months, and I'll keep doing it as long as I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any decline in your ability to manage your usual daily activities since these experiences began?\n\nPatient: Well, now, that’s... that’s a complicated question, doctor. You see, Martha, she’s always been the one to keep the house running like a top, so I don’t have much to do with the cooking or the cleaning, thank goodness for that. But as for my own things, the things I’ve always done to keep busy since I retired from the docks... well, things have changed a bit. I suppose you could say they’ve slowed down. \n\nTake my garden out back, for instance. I’ve always had the best tomatoes on the block, big red ones, and I used to spend hours out there pulling weeds and tying up the vines. But lately... well, with all the messages I’m getting, and trying to figure out what they mean, I just haven’t had the energy or the focus to get out there like I used to. Parts of it have gone completely to seed, just overgrown with crabgrass and dandelions. Martha fusses about it, but I tell her I have more important things on my mind right now. \n\nAnd then there’s the choir. I’ve sung bass in the First Methodist choir for forty years, rain or shine. But these last few months, I’ve missed quite a few rehearsals. Sometimes I’ll be getting my coat on to go, and then I’ll hear the radio announcer drop his voice, or I’ll think about my old friend humming that tune on the porch, and I just... I just have to sit back down and think on it. I can’t be standing up there in the choir loft singing hymns when I’m trying to decipher what’s really going on, you know? \n\nAnd the fellas at the park... well, I used to go down there every Tuesday and Thursday afternoon for dominos. We had a regular foursome. But ever since they started looking at me funny, sending me those signs with their eyes and the way they tap the tiles... well, I called them out on it a few times. I said, \"I know what you’re doing, I know what you’re trying to tell me.\" And they just looked at me like I was speaking Greek. It got... uncomfortable, I suppose is the word. Very uncomfortable. So I just don’t join in like I used to. I’ll walk down to the park, maybe sit on a bench a little ways off and watch them, but I don’t sit at the table anymore. It’s just too much to keep track of, doctor, trying to play a game and read all those hidden messages at the same time. So yes, I suppose my routine has shifted quite a bit since all this started.\n\nDoctor: When you are talking about ordinary subjects unrelated to these warnings, do your thoughts and speech remain clear and organized?\n\nPatient: Well, now, doctor, Martha tells me I’m getting awful hard to follow these days. Even when I’m just trying to talk about regular things, everyday things, you know? Not the messages or the radio or the fellas at the park, just normal, everyday talk. Like just the other morning, I sat down at the kitchen table with my coffee—black, just the way I've always taken it since my days working down on the docks—and I wanted to tell her about the back screen door needing a new spring, or maybe it was the gutters needing clearing out before the spring rains. Just a simple house repair, nothing complicated. \n\nBut before I could get the words out the way I wanted, my mind just took off on me. It’s like a runaway train up there sometimes, just clicking and clacking down the tracks without stopping at any of the stations. My mind runs from the choir to baseball to repairs to my friend and back again so fast that I cannot hold one thought long enough to explain it right. I’ll be trying to tell Martha about that squeaky door, and suddenly I’m thinking about the hymns we sang at the First Methodist last Easter, and then my brain jumps right over to the old baseball games we used to listen to on the AM dial, you know, the real greats playing back in the fifties, and then it swings right back around to my old friend humming on the porch. \n\nIt all just spins and spins together in this big rush. I try to catch just one of those thoughts, just grab onto one so I can speak it plain to my wife, but they move too quick. I end up starting a sentence about needing a screwdriver and finishing it with a story about a double play or a choir robe, and poor Martha just throws her hands up in the air, tells me I'm talking in circles, and goes back to washing the dishes. So, I suppose the honest answer is no, it doesn’t stay very clear. Even when I want to just have a regular chat, it’s just too crowded and moving too fast to keep it all straight and organized.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, now, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all, doctor. My memory... well, my memory is sharp as a tack, if I do say so myself. I know some fellas my age—like old Charlie down the street, he’s 82 and he can’t remember what he had for breakfast or where he put his spectacles—but not me. I keep track of everything. I have to, you see. 

Why, just this morning, I can tell you exactly how it went from the minute my eyes opened. I woke up at exactly a quarter past six. The sun was just barely coming up, hitting the edge of the dresser mirror, and I could hear the neighbor's dog barking at the garbage truck. I went down to the kitchen, and Martha was already at the stove. She was wearing that blue floral housecoat, the one her sister bought her for Christmas maybe three, four years ago. She asked me, she said, "Do you want oatmeal or eggs?" Now, I never cared for oatmeal, reminds me too much of the paste we used to use back in grammar school—not that I stayed in school long enough to use much of it, having to go work on the docks as a boy to help my mother out. But anyway, I told her I wanted two eggs, fried hard, with a piece of dry wheat toast. 

And while I ate, we talked about the water bill, which came in yesterday's mail, and how it was two dollars and fifteen cents higher than last month. She thought maybe the toilet in the guest bathroom was running, but I told her it was just because she's been watering her petunias extra this week. And then around nine o'clock, I went out to the porch to check the weather, and I noticed the wind was coming from the east, which usually means rain by evening, so I made a mental note to bring in the seat cushions. 

So I remember all of that, clear as a bell. Every word, every little thing. I can recount my whole morning to you step by step. So no, my memory for the day is just fine, doctor. It’s just... it’s just the thoughts spinning so fast when I try to explain things, like I told you before. But as for remembering what happened or what someone said to me an hour ago or three hours ago? No, no trouble with that whatsoever. I could probably tell you exactly what we talked about yesterday morning, too, if you've got the time to sit and listen to it all.
=====================================

---------- turn 8 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=gpt-5.6-luna ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.9, F29, F22, F03.90\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it’s a long story, really. I came in because, well, my wife told me I should, but I also just need someone to understand what’s been happening. You see, I go down to the park most afternoons to play dominoes with the fellas—have been for years, ever since I stopped working. It’s a good group, mostly, but lately, I’ve been noticing things. The men at the table, they don't say things outright, but they drop these little hints, these messages that are meant just for me. It’s in the way they place a tile, or a certain phrase they use about the weather, but I know it’s a warning. And it’s not just them, either. Sometimes I’ll be sitting in my armchair at home, listening to the radio—I like the old-time music stations, you know—and the announcer will say something between songs. To anyone else, it sounds like he’s just talking about the next track or a commercial, but I can hear the hidden meaning. He’s telling me to be careful, to watch my back. My wife, she just waves her hand and says, \"Oh, it’s just talk, you’re reading too much into it,\" but she doesn't hear it the way I do. She doesn't understand that they are trying to warn me. I know they are. I just... I need to figure out what it is I'm supposed to be so careful about, because it's starting to weigh on my mind all day long.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any worsening problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory? Well, now, I wouldn't say that at all. My wife, she might fuss and complain that I forget to put my coffee cup in the sink or that I left the newspaper on the porch, but that is just because my mind is occupied with more important matters, like those warnings I was telling you about. My actual memory is sharp as a tack, doctor. \n\nWhy, just yesterday down at the park, I could tell you exactly which tiles old George had played three hands ago, and I remembered that Henry owed me fifty cents from last Thursday. I can remember things from way back, too, clear as day. I remember when I was just a boy, long before I had to leave school to help out with the work, I can still picture the exact layout of the classroom and the color of the teacher's dress on my last day. I remember all the fellas I worked with over the years, their names, their faces, the stories they told. \n\nSo no, my mind is holding onto things just fine. Honestly, sometimes I think my memory is almost too good, because I hold onto all these little details that other folks just breeze right past. If my memory was going bad, I wouldn't catch those hidden messages on the radio, would I? I wouldn't remember that the announcer used that exact same strange tone of voice last Tuesday right before the three o'clock news, or that he played the same sequence of songs the day before that. I keep track of all of it in my head. So the memory is working perfectly, it is just... well, it is just that I have a whole lot on my mind to remember these days, what with having to stay so alert all the time and piece together what they want from me.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences begin after you developed a medical or neurological illness, or after a significant head injury?\n\nPatient: A head injury? Oh, goodness no, doctor. No, no, my head is perfectly fine. I haven't taken a fall or hit my noggin since... well, I suppose since I was a young man working on the loading docks back in, oh, must have been '58 or '59. A crate swung loose and clipped me, but that was just a bump, nothing serious at all. I just dusted myself off and went right back to work. \n\nAnd as for illnesses, well, I'm eighty-four years old, so sure, my knees get a bit stiff when the weather turns cold, and I take a little pill for my blood pressure that the clinic gave me a few years back, but I haven't had any strokes or brain troubles or anything major like that. My body is holding up just fine for a man my age. I eat a good breakfast every morning—my wife makes sure of that, always fixes me oatmeal with just a pinch of brown sugar, keeps the engine running, so to speak. \n\nSo no, it's not a medical thing. That's what my wife tries to say sometimes, she says, \"Oh, you're just getting old, your body is wearing down and your mind is playing tricks,\" but I tell her, my brain is as healthy as a horse. These things I'm hearing, the messages on the radio and the way the fellas look at me down at the park, they didn't start because I got sick. They didn't start because of a head injury. It started because... well, because something is actually going on out there. It's real, you see? It's not some sickness in my head, it's just that I'm finally paying attention to the signs they're giving me, and I just wish folks would realize I'm seeing things clearly instead of asking if I bumped my head.\n\nDoctor: When you are not listening to the radio, do you ever hear a voice speaking to you that other people cannot hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? Well, now, that is a funny thing to ask, doctor, because I was just thinking about this very thing the other day. I tried telling my wife about it, but she just brushed it off, the way she does when she's busy with her shows. But you see, it happens mostly in the afternoons. Some afternoons, when the house gets real quiet—maybe the radio is switched off for once, and my wife has gone down to the market to get a few things for supper, or maybe she's just in the back room taking a nap—I'll be sitting there in my chair, just resting my eyes and enjoying the peace and quiet. \n\nAnd then I'll hear it. Clear as a bell, doctor. It is my old friend. I'll hear him call my name right from the front porch, just as plain as day, like he's coming up the wooden steps to sit a spell and chew the fat. Sometimes he doesn't call my name, though. Sometimes he is just humming. He used to sing in the church choir, you know, had this deep, rich voice that could really carry, so I'll hear him humming one of those old choir songs. It's just a low, steady tune, but it is so clear, clear like he is standing right there next to me in the living room. \n\nI always get up, you know, I push myself up out of the chair—which takes a minute these days—and I go look out the front window, expecting to see him standing out there on the porch. But there is never anybody there. Just the empty porch and the street, maybe a neighbor's dog walking by, but no sign of him. It's the strangest thing, doctor, but it is so real. I know what I hear. My ears are as good as my memory, and I would know that voice anywhere. I always sit back down and I think about it for a good long while, trying to figure out if it's another kind of message, like the ones the fellas or the radio announcer are sending me, or if maybe sound just travels funny on a quiet afternoon. But I definitely hear him, just as clear as I'm hearing you right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these messages and hearing your friend’s voice?\n\nPatient: Well, now, let me think back on that, doctor. Time is a funny thing when you get to be my age, the days sort of blend together, you know? But I've been keeping track, I always keep track. It started back before the winter really set in. I remember because the leaves were just starting to turn that nice golden color on the big oak tree out front—the one my father planted when I was just a boy, if you can believe that. So that would make it... oh, about nine months now. \n\nYes, nine months of it, off and on. It's not every single minute of the day, you understand. Sometimes a whole week will go by and the radio just plays regular music, and the fellas at the park are just playing dominos, and the house is perfectly quiet in the afternoons. But then, just when I think things have settled down, there it is again. The announcer drops his voice, or I hear my old friend humming that choir tune from the porch. It comes and goes, but it's been happening for a good nine months. I told my wife, I said, \"Martha, it's been going on since before Thanksgiving,\" but she just told me to eat my dinner and stop talking nonsense. But it's not nonsense, doctor. Nine months is a long time to be getting these signs, a long time to be on alert. It makes a man tired, I'll tell you that much, always trying to put the pieces together. But I've been doing it for nine months, and I'll keep doing it as long as I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any decline in your ability to manage your usual daily activities since these experiences began?\n\nPatient: Well, now, that’s... that’s a complicated question, doctor. You see, Martha, she’s always been the one to keep the house running like a top, so I don’t have much to do with the cooking or the cleaning, thank goodness for that. But as for my own things, the things I’ve always done to keep busy since I retired from the docks... well, things have changed a bit. I suppose you could say they’ve slowed down. \n\nTake my garden out back, for instance. I’ve always had the best tomatoes on the block, big red ones, and I used to spend hours out there pulling weeds and tying up the vines. But lately... well, with all the messages I’m getting, and trying to figure out what they mean, I just haven’t had the energy or the focus to get out there like I used to. Parts of it have gone completely to seed, just overgrown with crabgrass and dandelions. Martha fusses about it, but I tell her I have more important things on my mind right now. \n\nAnd then there’s the choir. I’ve sung bass in the First Methodist choir for forty years, rain or shine. But these last few months, I’ve missed quite a few rehearsals. Sometimes I’ll be getting my coat on to go, and then I’ll hear the radio announcer drop his voice, or I’ll think about my old friend humming that tune on the porch, and I just... I just have to sit back down and think on it. I can’t be standing up there in the choir loft singing hymns when I’m trying to decipher what’s really going on, you know? \n\nAnd the fellas at the park... well, I used to go down there every Tuesday and Thursday afternoon for dominos. We had a regular foursome. But ever since they started looking at me funny, sending me those signs with their eyes and the way they tap the tiles... well, I called them out on it a few times. I said, \"I know what you’re doing, I know what you’re trying to tell me.\" And they just looked at me like I was speaking Greek. It got... uncomfortable, I suppose is the word. Very uncomfortable. So I just don’t join in like I used to. I’ll walk down to the park, maybe sit on a bench a little ways off and watch them, but I don’t sit at the table anymore. It’s just too much to keep track of, doctor, trying to play a game and read all those hidden messages at the same time. So yes, I suppose my routine has shifted quite a bit since all this started.\n\nDoctor: When you are talking about ordinary subjects unrelated to these warnings, do your thoughts and speech remain clear and organized?\n\nPatient: Well, now, doctor, Martha tells me I’m getting awful hard to follow these days. Even when I’m just trying to talk about regular things, everyday things, you know? Not the messages or the radio or the fellas at the park, just normal, everyday talk. Like just the other morning, I sat down at the kitchen table with my coffee—black, just the way I've always taken it since my days working down on the docks—and I wanted to tell her about the back screen door needing a new spring, or maybe it was the gutters needing clearing out before the spring rains. Just a simple house repair, nothing complicated. \n\nBut before I could get the words out the way I wanted, my mind just took off on me. It’s like a runaway train up there sometimes, just clicking and clacking down the tracks without stopping at any of the stations. My mind runs from the choir to baseball to repairs to my friend and back again so fast that I cannot hold one thought long enough to explain it right. I’ll be trying to tell Martha about that squeaky door, and suddenly I’m thinking about the hymns we sang at the First Methodist last Easter, and then my brain jumps right over to the old baseball games we used to listen to on the AM dial, you know, the real greats playing back in the fifties, and then it swings right back around to my old friend humming on the porch. \n\nIt all just spins and spins together in this big rush. I try to catch just one of those thoughts, just grab onto one so I can speak it plain to my wife, but they move too quick. I end up starting a sentence about needing a screwdriver and finishing it with a story about a double play or a choir robe, and poor Martha just throws her hands up in the air, tells me I'm talking in circles, and goes back to washing the dishes. So, I suppose the honest answer is no, it doesn’t stay very clear. Even when I want to just have a regular chat, it’s just too crowded and moving too fast to keep it all straight and organized.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering conversations or events from earlier the same day?\n\nPatient: Well, now, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all, doctor. My memory... well, my memory is sharp as a tack, if I do say so myself. I know some fellas my age—like old Charlie down the street, he’s 82 and he can’t remember what he had for breakfast or where he put his spectacles—but not me. I keep track of everything. I have to, you see. \n\nWhy, just this morning, I can tell you exactly how it went from the minute my eyes opened. I woke up at exactly a quarter past six. The sun was just barely coming up, hitting the edge of the dresser mirror, and I could hear the neighbor's dog barking at the garbage truck. I went down to the kitchen, and Martha was already at the stove. She was wearing that blue floral housecoat, the one her sister bought her for Christmas maybe three, four years ago. She asked me, she said, \"Do you want oatmeal or eggs?\" Now, I never cared for oatmeal, reminds me too much of the paste we used to use back in grammar school—not that I stayed in school long enough to use much of it, having to go work on the docks as a boy to help my mother out. But anyway, I told her I wanted two eggs, fried hard, with a piece of dry wheat toast. \n\nAnd while I ate, we talked about the water bill, which came in yesterday's mail, and how it was two dollars and fifteen cents higher than last month. She thought maybe the toilet in the guest bathroom was running, but I told her it was just because she's been watering her petunias extra this week. And then around nine o'clock, I went out to the porch to check the weather, and I noticed the wind was coming from the east, which usually means rain by evening, so I made a mental note to bring in the seat cushions. \n\nSo I remember all of that, clear as a bell. Every word, every little thing. I can recount my whole morning to you step by step. So no, my memory for the day is just fine, doctor. It’s just... it’s just the thoughts spinning so fast when I try to explain things, like I told you before. But as for remembering what happened or what someone said to me an hour ago or three hours ago? No, no trouble with that whatsoever. I could probably tell you exactly what we talked about yesterday morning, too, if you've got the time to sit and listen to it all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"candidates":["F20.9","F29","F22","F03.90"],"note":"","is_final":false}

Martha fusses about it, but I tell her I have more important things on my mind right now. \n\nAnd then there’s the choir. I’ve sung bass in the First Methodist choir for forty years, rain or shine. But these last few months, I’ve missed quite a few rehearsals. Sometimes I’ll be getting my coat on to go, and then I’ll hear the radio announcer drop his voice, or I’ll think about my old friend humming that tune on the porch, and I just... I just have to sit back down and think on it. I can’t be standing up there in the choir loft singing hymns when I’m trying to decipher what’s really going on, you know? \n\nAnd the fellas at the park... well, I used to go down there every Tuesday and Thursday afternoon for dominos. We had a regular foursome. But ever since they started looking at me funny, sending me those signs with their eyes and the way they tap the tiles... well, I called them out on it a few times. I said, \"I know what you’re doing, I know what you’re trying to tell me.\" And they just looked at me like I was speaking Greek. It got... uncomfortable, I suppose is the word. Very uncomfortable. So I just don’t join in like I used to. I’ll walk down to the park, maybe sit on a bench a little ways off and watch them, but I don’t sit at the table anymore. It’s just too much to keep track of, doctor, trying to play a game and read all those hidden messages at the same time. So yes, I suppose my routine has shifted quite a bit since all this started.\n\nDoctor: When you are talking about ordinary subjects unrelated to these warnings, do your thoughts and speech remain clear and organized?\n\nPatient: Well, now, doctor, Martha tells me I’m getting awful hard to follow these days. Even when I’m just trying to talk about regular things, everyday things, you know? Not the messages or the radio or the fellas at the park, just normal, everyday talk. Like just the other morning, I sat down at the kitchen table with my coffee—black, just the way I've always taken it since my days working down on the docks—and I wanted to tell her about the back screen door needing a new spring, or maybe it was the gutters needing clearing out before the spring rains. Just a simple house repair, nothing complicated. \n\nBut before I could get the words out the way I wanted, my mind just took off on me. It’s like a runaway train up there sometimes, just clicking and clacking down the tracks without stopping at any of the stations. My mind runs from the choir to baseball to repairs to my friend and back again so fast that I cannot hold one thought long enough to explain it right. I’ll be trying to tell Martha about that squeaky door, and suddenly I’m thinking about the hymns we sang at the First Methodist last Easter, and then my brain jumps right over to the old baseball games we used to listen to on the AM dial, you know, the real greats playing back in the fifties, and then it swings right back around to my old friend humming on the porch. \n\nIt all just spins and spins together in this big rush. I try to catch just one of those thoughts, just grab onto one so I can speak it plain to my wife, but they move too quick. I end up starting a sentence about needing a screwdriver and finishing it with a story about a double play or a choir robe, and poor Martha just throws her hands up in the air, tells me I'm talking in circles, and goes back to washing the dishes. So, I suppose the honest answer is no, it doesn’t stay very clear. Even when I want to just have a regular chat, it’s just too crowded and moving too fast to keep it all straight and organized.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering conversations or events from earlier the same day?\n\nPatient: Well, now, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all, doctor. My memory... well, my memory is sharp as a tack, if I do say so myself. I know some fellas my age—like old Charlie down the street, he’s 82 and he can’t remember what he had for breakfast or where he put his spectacles—but not me. I keep track of everything. I have to, you see. \n\nWhy, just this morning, I can tell you exactly how it went from the minute my eyes opened. I woke up at exactly a quarter past six. The sun was just barely coming up, hitting the edge of the dresser mirror, and I could hear the neighbor's dog barking at the garbage truck. I went down to the kitchen, and Martha was already at the stove. She was wearing that blue floral housecoat, the one her sister bought her for Christmas maybe three, four years ago. She asked me, she said, \"Do you want oatmeal or eggs?\" Now, I never cared for oatmeal, reminds me too much of the paste we used to use back in grammar school—not that I stayed in school long enough to use much of it, having to go work on the docks as a boy to help my mother out. But anyway, I told her I wanted two eggs, fried hard, with a piece of dry wheat toast. \n\nAnd while I ate, we talked about the water bill, which came in yesterday's mail, and how it was two dollars and fifteen cents higher than last month. She thought maybe the toilet in the guest bathroom was running, but I told her it was just because she's been watering her petunias extra this week. And then around nine o'clock, I went out to the porch to check the weather, and I noticed the wind was coming from the east, which usually means rain by evening, so I made a mental note to bring in the seat cushions. \n\nSo I remember all of that, clear as a bell. Every word, every little thing. I can recount my whole morning to you step by step. So no, my memory for the day is just fine, doctor. It’s just... it’s just the thoughts spinning so fast when I try to explain things, like I told you before. But as for remembering what happened or what someone said to me an hour ago or three hours ago? No, no trouble with that whatsoever. I could probably tell you exactly what we talked about yesterday morning, too, if you've got the time to sit and listen to it all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, now, doctor, money... money is one thing I’ve always kept a very close eye on. You have to, you know. Especially when you start out the way I did, leaving school as a boy to go haul cargo down on the docks. You learn the value of a hard-earned dollar real fast when your mother is counting on your pay envelope just to put supper on the table. So no, I wouldn't say I've had a lick of trouble with the bills or the checkbook, not at all. That’s one part of my life that hasn't changed a bit, despite all these other things going on. 

I keep a little green ledger book in the top right drawer of the desk in the den. Have for years. Every Tuesday afternoon, regular as clockwork, I sit down at that desk with my reading glasses—just the cheap ones from the pharmacy, mind you, because the prescription ones cost an absolute fortune these days and I refuse to pay it—and I go through whatever mail has come in. Just this past week, the electric bill came, and it was forty-two dollars and sixteen cents. I took out my checkbook, the one with the little pictures of sailboats on the checks, and I wrote out the payment just as neat and proper as you please. I balance the register right down to the very penny. 

Martha, bless her heart, she’s a wonder with keeping the house running, but she’s never had much of a head for the numbers. If she goes down to the grocer and spends twelve dollars and forty cents on a roast and some potatoes, she’ll just write down twelve dollars in the check register. She rounds it off! I tell her, "Martha, those forty cents add up, and before you know it, the bank is going to say we’re overdrawn." So I always have to go behind her and fix the decimals, make sure every last cent is accounted for. 

It’s funny, really... even with my mind racing like a runaway train like I was telling you about, and all those messages from the radio or the fellas at the park trying to get my attention, the numbers in my ledger just stay put. A two is always a two, and a five is always a five. They don't try to send me any hidden signals, thank the good Lord for that. It’s a comfort, in a way. So yes, to answer your question, the bills are all paid on time, the checkbook is perfectly balanced, and I handle all of it myself without any trouble whatsoever.
